     1→---
     2→name: helion-triton-code-generation
     3→description: |
     4→  Workflow for using Helion (pytorch/helion) locally to generate Triton GPU kernel code.
     5→  Use when: (1) need to discover correct Triton tl.dot_scaled scale layout for MXFP4,
     6→  (2) want to bootstrap a Triton kernel from a high-level PyTorch-like DSL, (3) exploring
     7→  what Triton ops hardware supports before writing low-level kernel manually.
     8→  Helion is NOT available on AMD speedrun runners — local code generation only.
     9→author: Claude Code
    10→version: 1.0.0
    11→tags: [helion, triton, code-generation, mxfp4, amd, gpu-kernels]
    12→---
    13→
    14→# Helion → Triton Code Generation Workflow
    15→
    16→## Problem
    17→
    18→Writing Triton MXFP4 kernels for AMD gfx950 requires understanding correct tile shapes,
    19→scale layouts, and hardware constraints — none of which are documented. Helion (a PyTorch DSL
    20→that compiles to Triton) can generate valid Triton code that can be read and adapted.
    21→
    22→## Context
    23→
    24→**Helion v0.3.1** is a PyTorch DSL by the PyTorch team that compiles `@helion.kernel`
    25→decorated functions to Triton code. It knows about hardware-specific constraints and
    26→generates correct `tl.dot_scaled` calls including the proper `"e2m1"` format string.
    27→
    28→**Availability:** Helion is NOT pre-installed on AMD speedrun runners. Install and use locally
    29→only for code generation.
    30→
    31→## Setup: Isolated CPU venv
    32→
    33→Create an isolated venv OUTSIDE the project (avoid uv's lockfile overwriting torch):
    34→
    35→```bash
    36→python3 -m venv /path/to/project/.venv-helion
    37→source /path/to/project/.venv-helion/bin/activate
    38→
    39→# Install CPU-only torch + Helion
    40→pip install helion packaging torch --index-url https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cpu
    41→```
    42→
    43→**Critical:** Do NOT use `uv run` or `uv venv` — the project's lockfile pins CUDA/ROCm torch
    44→and will overwrite a manually installed Helion. Use a standalone `python3 -m venv` instead.
    45→
    46→## Writing a Helion Kernel
    47→
    48→**CRITICAL:** Write kernel to a `.py` FILE, not stdin/heredoc.
    49→Helion uses `inspect.getsource()` which fails on non-file modules (raises `OSError: could not get source code`).
    50→
    51→```python
    52→# helion_gemm_test.py — must be a real file, not inline
    53→import torch
    54→import helion
    55→import helion.language as hl
    56→
    57→@helion.kernel
    58→def mxfp4_gemm(
    59→    A: torch.Tensor,       # [M, K//2] packed fp4 (uint8)
    60→    A_scale: torch.Tensor, # [M, K//32] e8m0 scales (uint8)
    61→    B: torch.Tensor,       # [K//2, N] packed fp4 (uint8), B transposed
    62→    B_scale: torch.Tensor, # [N, K//32] e8m0 scales (uint8)
    63→) -> torch.Tensor:
    64→    M, K_HALF = A.shape
    65→    _, N = B.shape
    66→    C = hl.zeros([M, N], dtype=torch.bfloat16)
    67→    for tile_m, tile_n in hl.tile([M, N]):
    68→        acc = hl.zeros([tile_m, tile_n], dtype=torch.float32)
    69→        for tile_k in hl.tile([K_HALF]):
    70→            a_tile = A[tile_m, tile_k]
    71→            a_scale_tile = A_scale[tile_m, tile_k // 16]
    72→            b_tile = B[tile_k, tile_n]
    73→            b_scale_tile = B_scale[tile_n, tile_k // 16]
    74→            acc = hl.dot_scaled(a_tile, a_scale_tile, "e2m1", b_tile, b_scale_tile, "e2m1", acc=acc)
    75→        C[tile_m, tile_n] = acc.to(torch.bfloat16)
    76→    return C
    77→```
    78→
    79→## Generating Triton Code
    80→
    81→```python
    82→# In helion_gemm_test.py (continued)
    83→from helion.runtime.debug import to_triton_code
    84→
    85→# Create dummy inputs that match the real shape/dtype
    86→m, k_half, n = 64, 256, 128
    87→A = torch.zeros(m, k_half, dtype=torch.uint8)
    88→A_scale = torch.zeros(m, k_half // 16, dtype=torch.uint8)
    89→B = torch.zeros(k_half, n, dtype=torch.uint8)
    90→B_scale = torch.zeros(n, k_half // 16, dtype=torch.uint8)
    91→
    92→code = to_triton_code(mxfp4_gemm, (A, A_scale, B, B_scale))
    93→print(code)
    94→```
    95→
    96→Run with:
    97→```bash
    98→source .venv-helion/bin/activate
    99→python helion_gemm_test.py
   100→```
   101→
   102→The generated code appears in stdout AND in a temp file under `/tmp/torchinductor_<user>/`:
   103→```bash
   104→ls -la /tmp/torchinductor_*/  # Find generated .py files
   105→```
   106→
   107→## Reading the Generated Code
   108→
   109→The generated Triton code shows:
   110→- Correct `tl.dot_scaled(a, a_scale, "e2m1", b, b_scale, "e2m1", acc=acc)` call syntax
   111→- Scale tensor load patterns (which dimension is outer/inner)
   112→- Correct `constexpr` for `SCALE_PER_BLOCK = BLOCK_K // 16`
   113→- Proper mask handling for boundary tiles
   114→
   115→## Key Patterns Discovered
   116→
   117→From Helion-generated MXFP4 GEMM code:
   118→- `tl.dot_scaled` format string is `"e2m1"` (not `"fp4_e2m1"` or `"mxfp4"`)
   119→- LHS scale is `[BLOCK_M, BLOCK_K // 16]`
   120→- RHS scale is `[BLOCK_N, BLOCK_K // 16]` (N-first, matching B_scale storage layout)
   121→- `SCALE_PER_BLOCK: tl.constexpr = BLOCK_K // 16` — this is a constexpr
   122→
   123→## Adapting for Competition Format
   124→
   125→After generating code, adapt to dynamic shapes:
   126→1. Replace `constexpr` tile sizes with `BLOCK_M: tl.constexpr` kernel parameters
   127→2. Add boundary masks: `mask_m = offs_m < M`
   128→3. Handle padded loads with `other=0`
   129→4. Replace Helion's auto-grid with explicit grid calculation: `triton.cdiv(m, BLOCK_M)`
   130→5. Pass strides explicitly for non-contiguous tensors
   131→
   132→## Troubleshooting
   133→
   134→| Error | Cause | Fix |
   135→|-------|-------|-----|
   136→| `OSError: could not get source code` | Kernel defined in stdin/REPL/heredoc | Write to `.py` file |
   137→| `ModuleNotFoundError: helion` | Wrong venv active | `source .venv-helion/bin/activate` |
   138→| `uv run` overwrites torch | uv lockfile pins CUDA torch | Use `python3` directly, not `uv run` |
   139→| `AttributeError: module 'helion' has no 'kernel'` | Old helion version | `pip install --upgrade helion` |
   140→
   141→## Context
   142→
   143→Discovered during Luma AMD Speedrun competition (March 2026). Used to reverse-engineer the
   144→correct `tl.dot_scaled` scale layout for MXFP4 on AMD gfx950 (MI355X). The Helion-generated
   145→kernel structure was adapted into `submission_triton_dotscaled.py`.
   146→
   147→Helion v0.3.1, pytorch/helion repository. Requires only CPU torch locally (no GPU).
   148→

### Matrix Multiplication Autotuning Kernel in Python with Helion

Source: https://github.com/pytorch/helion/blob/main/docs/helion_puzzles.rst

Demonstrates matrix multiplication using Helion's autotuning feature. The `@helion.kernel()` decorator without configuration enables autotuning, which optimizes the kernel's performance based on the hardware. This example requires larger tensors for effective autotuning. Dependencies include PyTorch, Helion, and time.

```python
import torch
import helion
import helion.language as hl
import time

# Define a matrix multiplication kernel
@helion.kernel()  # No config means autotuning will be used
def matmul_autotune(x: torch.Tensor, y: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
    m, k = x.size()
    k, n = y.size()
    out = torch.empty([m, n], dtype=x.dtype, device=x.device)

    for tile_m, tile_n in hl.tile([m, n]):
        acc = hl.zeros([tile_m, tile_n], dtype=torch.float32)
        for tile_k in hl.tile(k):
            acc = acc + torch.matmul(x[tile_m, tile_k], y[tile_k, tile_n])
        out[tile_m, tile_n] = acc

    return out

# Create larger tensors for better autotuning results
x = torch.randn(1024, 1024, device="cuda")
y = torch.randn(1024, 1024, device="cuda")

# First run will trigger autotuning
print("Running with autotuning (this might take a while)...")
start = time.time()
```

### FlashAttention Kernel Implementation in PyTorch

Source: https://github.com/pytorch/helion/blob/main/docs/helion_puzzles.rst

Implements the FlashAttention mechanism using Helion kernels for efficient computation. It processes queries, keys, and values in tiles to optimize cache usage and numerical stability during softmax calculation.

```python
    @helion.kernel()
    def flashatt_kernel(q: torch.Tensor, k: torch.Tensor, v: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
        # Get tensor size
        seq_len = q.size(0)
        # Create output tensor
        out = torch.empty_like(q)

        # Process each query position
        for tile_q in hl.tile(seq_len):
            q_tile = q[tile_q]

            # Initialize tracking variables for stable softmax
            max_val = torch.full_like(q_tile, float('-inf'))
            sum_exp = torch.zeros_like(q_tile)
            weighted_sum = torch.zeros_like(q_tile)

            # Process in tiles for better cache efficiency
            for tile_kv in hl.tile(seq_len):
                k_tile = k[tile_kv]
                v_tile = v[tile_kv]

                # Compute attention scores
                scores = q_tile[:, None] * k_tile[None, :]

                # Find max for numerical stability
                batch_max = torch.amax(scores, dim=1)
                new_max = torch.maximum(max_val, batch_max)

                # Scale old accumulations
                scale_factor = torch.exp(max_val - new_max)
                sum_exp = sum_exp * scale_factor
                weighted_sum = weighted_sum * scale_factor

                # Update with new values
                exp_scores = torch.exp(scores - new_max[:, None])
                sum_exp = sum_exp + torch.sum(exp_scores, dim=1)
                weighted_sum = weighted_sum + torch.sum(exp_scores * v_tile[None, :], dim=1)

                # Update max_val
                max_val = new_max

            # Compute final output
            out[tile_q] = weighted_sum / sum_exp

        return out
```

--------------------------------

### Minimal Matrix Multiplication Kernel in Helion and PyTorch

Source: https://github.com/pytorch/helion/blob/main/docs/index.md

This Python code defines a matrix multiplication kernel using Helion. It leverages PyTorch for tensor operations and CPU-bound logic, while Helion compiles the core computation within tiled loops into a single GPU kernel. The function takes two PyTorch tensors as input and returns their product.

```python
import torch, helion, helion.language as hl

@helion.kernel()
def matmul(x: torch.Tensor, y: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
    m, k = x.size()
    k2, n = y.size()
    assert k == k2, f"size mismatch {k} != {k2}"
    out = torch.empty([m, n], dtype=x.dtype, device=x.device)

    for tile_m, tile_n in hl.tile([m, n]):
        acc = hl.zeros([tile_m, tile_n], dtype=torch.float32)
        for tile_k in hl.tile(k):
            acc = torch.addmm(acc, x[tile_m, tile_k], y[tile_k, tile_n])
        out[tile_m, tile_n] = acc

    return out
```

--------------------------------

### Implement Matrix Multiplication with Tiled Iteration

Source: https://context7.com/pytorch/helion/llms.txt

Implements a matrix multiplication kernel using `hl.tile()` for both output dimensions and the reduction dimension. `hl.tile()` subdivides iteration spaces into parallelizable GPU tiles. This function takes two PyTorch tensors (matrices) and returns their product.

```python
import torch
import helion
import helion.language as hl

@helion.kernel()
def matmul(x: torch.Tensor, y: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
    m, k = x.size()
    k, n = y.size()
    out = torch.empty([m, n], dtype=x.dtype, device=x.device)

    # 2D tiling over output dimensions
    for tile_m, tile_n in hl.tile([m, n]):
        acc = hl.zeros([tile_m, tile_n], dtype=torch.float32)
        # 1D tiling over reduction dimension
        for tile_k in hl.tile(k):
            acc = torch.addmm(acc, x[tile_m, tile_k], y[tile_k, tile_n])
        out[tile_m, tile_n] = acc

    return out

# Usage
a = torch.randn(512, 256, device="cuda", dtype=torch.float16)
b = torch.randn(256, 512, device="cuda", dtype=torch.float16)
c = matmul(a, b)
```

     1→"""
     2→MLA decode: Triton flash-decode kernel with online softmax + aiter ASM hybrid.
     3→
     4→Strategy:
     5→- Small shapes (total_kv <= 32k): Triton flash-decode with bf16 KV
     6→  - Avoids aiter metadata/reduce overhead for small workloads
     7→  - Online softmax, no materialized attention matrix
     8→- Medium shapes (32k < total_kv <= 256k): Triton flash-decode with fp8 KV
     9→  - 2x bandwidth savings from fp8 KV cache
    10→  - Still single-pass (no split-k reduce kernel)
    11→- Large shapes (total_kv > 256k): aiter ASM with adaptive splits
    12→  - ASM kernels are heavily optimized for large sequences on gfx950
    13→  - Split-k with intra_batch_mode amortizes reduce overhead
    14→
    15→Grid: (batch_size, num_heads) -- one program per (batch, head) pair.
    16→Each program streams over the KV sequence with online softmax.
    17→
    18→Head dim 576 is NOT a power of 2 — we pad to 640 (next multiple of 64) for
    19→aligned loads. The extra 64 dims are masked to zero. V_DIM=512 is already
    20→a power of 2 and needs no padding.
    21→"""
    22→import torch
    23→import triton
    24→import triton.language as tl
    25→from task import input_t, output_t
    26→from reference import ref_kernel
    27→
    28→# Try importing aiter for the large-shape ASM path
    29→try:
    30→    from aiter.mla import mla_decode_fwd
    31→    from aiter import dtypes as aiter_dtypes
    32→    from aiter import get_mla_metadata_info_v1, get_mla_metadata_v1
    33→    HAS_AITER = True
    34→    FP8_DTYPE = aiter_dtypes.fp8
    35→except ImportError:
    36→    HAS_AITER = False
    37→    FP8_DTYPE = None
    38→
    39→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    40→# MLA constants (DeepSeek R1 forward_absorb)
    41→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    42→QK_HEAD_DIM = 576
    43→QK_PAD_DIM = 640          # 576 padded to next multiple of 64
    44→V_HEAD_DIM = 512
    45→NUM_KV_HEADS = 1
    46→SM_SCALE: float = 1.0 / (QK_HEAD_DIM ** 0.5)
    47→PAGE_SIZE = 1
    48→
    49→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    50→# Routing thresholds (total_kv = batch_size * kv_seq_len)
    51→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    52→TRITON_BF16_THRESHOLD = 32_768      # <= 32k tokens: Triton bf16
    53→TRITON_FP8_THRESHOLD = 262_144      # <= 256k tokens: Triton fp8
    54→# > 256k tokens: aiter ASM
    55→
    56→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    57→# Caches
    58→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    59→_aiter_cache: dict = {}
    60→
    61→
    62→# ===========================================================================
    63→# Triton flash-decode kernel (bf16 KV path)
    64→# ===========================================================================
    65→@triton.jit
    66→def _mla_flash_decode_bf16(
    67→    Q_ptr, KV_ptr, Out_ptr,
    68→    stride_qb, stride_qh, stride_qd,
    69→    stride_kvb, stride_kvs, stride_kvd,
    70→    stride_ob, stride_oh, stride_od,
    71→    sm_scale,
    72→    kvseqlen,
    73→    BLOCK_N: tl.constexpr,
    74→    QK_REAL: tl.constexpr,
    75→    QK_PAD: tl.constexpr,
    76→    V_DIM: tl.constexpr,
    77→):
    78→    """Flash-decode for one (batch, head) pair with bf16 KV cache.
    79→
    80→    Q:   [bs, nheads, QK_REAL] bf16
    81→    KV:  [bs, kvseqlen, QK_REAL] bf16
    82→    Out: [bs, nheads, V_DIM] bf16
    83→    """
    84→    bid = tl.program_id(0)
    85→    hid = tl.program_id(1)
    86→
    87→    # Load full query vector into registers (padded to QK_PAD)
    88→    offs_qk = tl.arange(0, QK_PAD)
    89→    qk_mask = offs_qk < QK_REAL
    90→    q = tl.load(
    91→        Q_ptr + bid * stride_qb + hid * stride_qh + offs_qk * stride_qd,
    92→        mask=qk_mask,
    93→        other=0.0,
    94→    ).to(tl.float32)
    95→
    96→    # Online softmax accumulators
    97→    m_i = -float("inf")
    98→    l_i = 0.0
    99→    offs_v = tl.arange(0, V_DIM)
   100→    acc = tl.zeros([V_DIM], dtype=tl.float32)
   101→
   102→    # Stream over KV sequence in BLOCK_N chunks
   103→    for start_n in range(0, kvseqlen, BLOCK_N):
   104→        offs_n = start_n + tl.arange(0, BLOCK_N)
   105→        mask_n = offs_n < kvseqlen
   106→
   107→        # Load K block: [BLOCK_N, QK_PAD] with dim mask
   108→        k = tl.load(
   109→            KV_ptr
   110→            + bid * stride_kvb
   111→            + offs_n[:, None] * stride_kvs
   112→            + offs_qk[None, :] * stride_kvd,
   113→            mask=mask_n[:, None] & qk_mask[None, :],
   114→            other=0.0,
   115→        ).to(tl.float32)
   116→
   117→        # QK scores: q[1, QK_PAD] * k[BLOCK_N, QK_PAD] -> sum -> [BLOCK_N]
   118→        qk = tl.sum(q[None, :] * k, axis=1) * sm_scale
   119→        qk = tl.where(mask_n, qk, -float("inf"))
   120→
   121→        # Online softmax update
   122→        m_ij = tl.max(qk, axis=0)
   123→        p = tl.exp(qk - m_ij)
   124→        l_ij = tl.sum(p, axis=0)
   125→
   126→        m_new = tl.maximum(m_i, m_ij)
   127→        alpha = tl.exp(m_i - m_new)
   128→        beta = tl.exp(m_ij - m_new)
   129→
   130→        l_i = l_i * alpha + l_ij * beta
   131→        acc = acc * alpha
   132→
   133→        # Load V block: [BLOCK_N, V_DIM] (first 512 dims of KV buffer)
   134→        v = tl.load(
   135→            KV_ptr
   136→            + bid * stride_kvb
   137→            + offs_n[:, None] * stride_kvs
   138→            + offs_v[None, :] * stride_kvd,
   139→            mask=mask_n[:, None],
   140→            other=0.0,
   141→        ).to(tl.float32)
   142→
   143→        # Weighted value accumulation
   144→        acc += tl.sum(p[:, None] * v, axis=0) * beta
   145→        m_i = m_new
   146→
   147→    # Normalize and store
   148→    out = (acc / l_i).to(tl.bfloat16)
   149→    tl.store(
   150→        Out_ptr + bid * stride_ob + hid * stride_oh + offs_v * stride_od,
   151→        out,
   152→    )
   153→
   154→
   155→# ===========================================================================
   156→# Triton flash-decode kernel (fp8 KV path)
   157→# ===========================================================================
   158→@triton.jit
   159→def _mla_flash_decode_fp8(
   160→    Q_ptr, KV_ptr, Out_ptr,
   161→    stride_qb, stride_qh, stride_qd,
   162→    stride_kvb, stride_kvs, stride_kvd,
   163→    stride_ob, stride_oh, stride_od,
   164→    sm_scale,
   165→    kv_scale,
   166→    kvseqlen,
   167→    BLOCK_N: tl.constexpr,
   168→    QK_REAL: tl.constexpr,
   169→    QK_PAD: tl.constexpr,
   170→    V_DIM: tl.constexpr,
   171→):
   172→    """Flash-decode with fp8 KV cache — 2x bandwidth savings.
   173→
   174→    Q:   [bs, nheads, QK_REAL] bf16
   175→    KV:  [bs, kvseqlen, QK_REAL] fp8
   176→    Out: [bs, nheads, V_DIM] bf16
   177→
   178→    kv_scale dequantizes fp8 values. For QK dot product we fold kv_scale into
   179→    sm_scale (effective_sm_scale = sm_scale * kv_scale). For V accumulation we
   180→    apply kv_scale after the final normalization.
   181→    """
   182→    bid = tl.program_id(0)
   183→    hid = tl.program_id(1)
   184→
   185→    # Load Q into registers (padded to QK_PAD, masked)
   186→    offs_qk = tl.arange(0, QK_PAD)
   187→    qk_mask = offs_qk < QK_REAL
   188→    q = tl.load(
   189→        Q_ptr + bid * stride_qb + hid * stride_qh + offs_qk * stride_qd,
   190→        mask=qk_mask,
   191→        other=0.0,
   192→    ).to(tl.float32)
   193→
   194→    # Fold kv_scale into QK scaling so we don't need a separate multiply
   195→    effective_sm_scale = sm_scale * kv_scale
   196→
   197→    # Online softmax accumulators
   198→    m_i = -float("inf")
   199→    l_i = 0.0
   200→    offs_v = tl.arange(0, V_DIM)
   201→    acc = tl.zeros([V_DIM], dtype=tl.float32)
   202→
   203→    for start_n in range(0, kvseqlen, BLOCK_N):
   204→        offs_n = start_n + tl.arange(0, BLOCK_N)
   205→        mask_n = offs_n < kvseqlen
   206→
   207→        # Load fp8 K block, cast to f32
   208→        k = tl.load(
   209→            KV_ptr
   210→            + bid * stride_kvb
   211→            + offs_n[:, None] * stride_kvs
   212→            + offs_qk[None, :] * stride_kvd,
   213→            mask=mask_n[:, None] & qk_mask[None, :],
   214→            other=0.0,
   215→        ).to(tl.float32)
   216→
   217→        # QK scores with kv_scale folded in
   218→        qk = tl.sum(q[None, :] * k, axis=1) * effective_sm_scale
   219→        qk = tl.where(mask_n, qk, -float("inf"))
   220→
   221→        # Online softmax update
   222→        m_ij = tl.max(qk, axis=0)
   223→        p = tl.exp(qk - m_ij)
   224→        l_ij = tl.sum(p, axis=0)
   225→
   226→        m_new = tl.maximum(m_i, m_ij)
   227→        alpha = tl.exp(m_i - m_new)
   228→        beta = tl.exp(m_ij - m_new)
   229→
   230→        l_i = l_i * alpha + l_ij * beta
   231→        acc = acc * alpha
   232→
   233→        # Load fp8 V block, cast to f32
   234→        v = tl.load(
   235→            KV_ptr
   236→            + bid * stride_kvb
   237→            + offs_n[:, None] * stride_kvs
   238→            + offs_v[None, :] * stride_kvd,
   239→            mask=mask_n[:, None],
   240→            other=0.0,
   241→        ).to(tl.float32)
   242→
   243→        # V not scaled here — we apply kv_scale after normalization
   244→        acc += tl.sum(p[:, None] * v, axis=0) * beta
   245→        m_i = m_new
   246→
   247→    # Normalize, then apply kv_scale to V accumulation
   248→    out = (acc * kv_scale / l_i).to(tl.bfloat16)
   249→    tl.store(
   250→        Out_ptr + bid * stride_ob + hid * stride_oh + offs_v * stride_od,
   251→        out,
   252→    )
   253→
   254→
   255→# ===========================================================================
   256→# Aiter ASM path (large shapes) — from submission_adaptive_splits pattern
   257→# ===========================================================================
   258→def _choose_num_kv_splits(total_kv: int) -> int:
   259→    """Adaptive split count: fewer splits for small workloads."""
   260→    if total_kv <= 8192:
   261→        return 1
   262→    if total_kv <= 65536:
   263→        return 8
   264→    if total_kv <= 524288:
   265→        return 16
   266→    return 32
   267→
   268→
   269→def _quantize_fp8(tensor: torch.Tensor):
   270→    """Dynamic per-tensor FP8 quantization."""
   271→    finfo = torch.finfo(FP8_DTYPE)
   272→    amax = tensor.abs().amax().clamp(min=1e-12)
   273→    scale = amax / finfo.max
   274→    return (
   275→        (tensor / scale).clamp(finfo.min, finfo.max).to(FP8_DTYPE),
   276→        scale.float().reshape(1),
   277→    )
   278→
   279→
   280→def _build_aiter_cache(
   281→    bs: int,
   282→    qseqlen: int,
   283→    kvseqlen: int,
   284→    nheads: int,
   285→    q_dtype,
   286→    kv_dtype,
   287→    num_kv_splits: int,
   288→    qo_indptr: torch.Tensor,
   289→    kv_indptr: torch.Tensor,
   290→) -> dict:
   291→    """Build and cache aiter persistent-mode metadata buffers."""
   292→    total_kv = bs * kvseqlen
   293→    kv_indices = torch.arange(total_kv, dtype=torch.int32, device="cuda")
   294→    kv_last_page_len = (kv_indptr[1:] - kv_indptr[:-1]).to(torch.int32)
   295→
   296→    info = get_mla_metadata_info_v1(
   297→        bs, qseqlen, nheads, q_dtype, kv_dtype,
   298→        is_sparse=False, fast_mode=True,
   299→        num_kv_splits=num_kv_splits, intra_batch_mode=True,
   300→    )
   301→    wm, wi, wis, ri, rfm, rpm = [
   302→        torch.empty(s, dtype=t, device="cuda") for s, t in info
   303→    ]
   304→
   305→    get_mla_metadata_v1(
   306→        qo_indptr, kv_indptr, kv_last_page_len,
   307→        nheads // NUM_KV_HEADS, NUM_KV_HEADS, True,
   308→        wm, wis, wi, ri, rfm, rpm,
   309→        page_size=PAGE_SIZE,
   310→        kv_granularity=max(PAGE_SIZE, 16),
   311→        max_seqlen_qo=qseqlen, uni_seqlen_qo=qseqlen,
   312→        fast_mode=True,
   313→        max_split_per_batch=num_kv_splits,
   314→        intra_batch_mode=True,
   315→        dtype_q=q_dtype, dtype_kv=kv_dtype,
   316→    )
   317→
   318→    return {
   319→        "kv_indices": kv_indices,
   320→        "kv_last_page_len": kv_last_page_len,
   321→        "work_meta_data": wm,
   322→        "work_indptr": wi,
   323→        "work_info_set": wis,
   324→        "reduce_indptr": ri,
   325→        "reduce_final_map": rfm,
   326→        "reduce_partial_map": rpm,
   327→    }
   328→
   329→
   330→def _run_aiter_asm(
   331→    q: torch.Tensor,
   332→    kv_data: dict,
   333→    qo_indptr: torch.Tensor,
   334→    kv_indptr: torch.Tensor,
   335→    config: dict,
   336→) -> torch.Tensor:
   337→    """Aiter ASM MLA decode with adaptive splits and a16w8 dtype routing."""
   338→    bs = config["batch_size"]
   339→    kvseqlen = config["kv_seq_len"]
   340→    nheads = config["num_heads"]
   341→    total_kv = bs * kvseqlen
   342→
   343→    # a16w8 (bf16 Q + fp8 KV) for medium shapes; a8w8 for very large
   344→    use_a16w8 = total_kv <= 262144
   345→    num_kv_splits = _choose_num_kv_splits(total_kv)
   346→
   347→    kv_fp8, kv_scale = kv_data["fp8"]
   348→    kv_4d = kv_fp8.view(
   349→        kv_fp8.shape[0], PAGE_SIZE, NUM_KV_HEADS, kv_fp8.shape[-1]
   350→    )
   351→
   352→    if use_a16w8:
   353→        q_input = q
   354→        q_scale = None
   355→        q_dtype = torch.bfloat16
   356→    else:
   357→        q_input, q_scale = _quantize_fp8(q)
   358→        q_dtype = FP8_DTYPE
   359→
   360→    key = (bs, kvseqlen, nheads, use_a16w8, num_kv_splits)
   361→    if key not in _aiter_cache:
   362→        _aiter_cache[key] = _build_aiter_cache(
   363→            bs, 1, kvseqlen, nheads,
   364→            q_dtype, FP8_DTYPE, num_kv_splits,
   365→            qo_indptr, kv_indptr,
   366→        )
   367→    c = _aiter_cache[key]
   368→
   369→    o = torch.empty(
   370→        (q.shape[0], nheads, V_HEAD_DIM),
   371→        dtype=torch.bfloat16, device="cuda",
   372→    )
   373→
   374→    mla_decode_fwd(
   375→        q_input.view(-1, nheads, QK_HEAD_DIM), kv_4d, o,
   376→        qo_indptr, kv_indptr,
   377→        c["kv_indices"], c["kv_last_page_len"],
   378→        1,
   379→        page_size=PAGE_SIZE, nhead_kv=NUM_KV_HEADS,
   380→        sm_scale=SM_SCALE, logit_cap=0.0,
   381→        num_kv_splits=num_kv_splits,
   382→        q_scale=q_scale, kv_scale=kv_scale,
   383→        intra_batch_mode=True,
   384→        work_meta_data=c["work_meta_data"],
   385→        work_indptr=c["work_indptr"],
   386→        work_info_set=c["work_info_set"],
   387→        reduce_indptr=c["reduce_indptr"],
   388→        reduce_final_map=c["reduce_final_map"],
   389→        reduce_partial_map=c["reduce_partial_map"],
   390→    )
   391→    return o
   392→
   393→
   394→# ===========================================================================
   395→# Triton launch helpers
   396→# ===========================================================================
   397→def _triton_bf16_decode(
   398→    q: torch.Tensor,
   399→    kv_bf16: torch.Tensor,
   400→    bs: int,
   401→    kvseqlen: int,
   402→    nheads: int,
   403→) -> torch.Tensor:
   404→    """Launch Triton flash-decode with bf16 KV for small shapes."""
   405→    # Squeeze num_kv_heads=1 dim: (total_kv, 1, 576) -> (bs, kvseqlen, 576)
   406→    kv = kv_bf16.view(bs, kvseqlen, QK_HEAD_DIM)
   407→    q_view = q.view(bs, nheads, QK_HEAD_DIM)
   408→
   409→    out = torch.empty(
   410→        (bs, nheads, V_HEAD_DIM), dtype=torch.bfloat16, device=q.device
   411→    )
   412→
   413→    # Tune BLOCK_N and warps for the sequence length
   414→    if kvseqlen <= 1024:
   415→        block_n = 64
   416→        num_warps = 4
   417→    else:
   418→        block_n = 128
   419→        num_warps = 8
   420→
   421→    grid = (bs, nheads)
   422→    _mla_flash_decode_bf16[grid](
   423→        q_view, kv, out,
   424→        q_view.stride(0), q_view.stride(1), q_view.stride(2),
   425→        kv.stride(0), kv.stride(1), kv.stride(2),
   426→        out.stride(0), out.stride(1), out.stride(2),
   427→        SM_SCALE,
   428→        kvseqlen,
   429→        BLOCK_N=block_n,
   430→        QK_REAL=QK_HEAD_DIM,
   431→        QK_PAD=QK_PAD_DIM,
   432→        V_DIM=V_HEAD_DIM,
   433→        num_warps=num_warps,
   434→        num_stages=1,
   435→    )
   436→    return out
   437→
   438→
   439→def _triton_fp8_decode(
   440→    q: torch.Tensor,
   441→    kv_fp8: torch.Tensor,
   442→    kv_scale: torch.Tensor,
   443→    bs: int,
   444→    kvseqlen: int,
   445→    nheads: int,
   446→) -> torch.Tensor:
   447→    """Launch Triton flash-decode with fp8 KV for medium shapes."""
   448→    kv = kv_fp8.view(bs, kvseqlen, QK_HEAD_DIM)
   449→    q_view = q.view(bs, nheads, QK_HEAD_DIM)
   450→
   451→    out = torch.empty(
   452→        (bs, nheads, V_HEAD_DIM), dtype=torch.bfloat16, device=q.device
   453→    )
   454→
   455→    # Extract scalar for passing to Triton (avoids pointer indirection)
   456→    kv_scale_val = kv_scale.item()
   457→
   458→    block_n = 128
   459→    num_warps = 8
   460→
   461→    grid = (bs, nheads)
   462→    _mla_flash_decode_fp8[grid](
   463→        q_view, kv, out,
   464→        q_view.stride(0), q_view.stride(1), q_view.stride(2),
   465→        kv.stride(0), kv.stride(1), kv.stride(2),
   466→        out.stride(0), out.stride(1), out.stride(2),
   467→        SM_SCALE,
   468→        kv_scale_val,
   469→        kvseqlen,
   470→        BLOCK_N=block_n,
   471→        QK_REAL=QK_HEAD_DIM,
   472→        QK_PAD=QK_PAD_DIM,
   473→        V_DIM=V_HEAD_DIM,
   474→        num_warps=num_warps,
   475→        num_stages=1,
   476→    )
   477→    return out
   478→
   479→
   480→# ===========================================================================
   481→# Entry point
   482→# ===========================================================================
   483→def custom_kernel(data: input_t) -> output_t:
   484→    q, kv_data, qo_indptr, kv_indptr, config = data
   485→    bs = config["batch_size"]
   486→    kvseqlen = config["kv_seq_len"]
   487→    qseqlen = config["q_seq_len"]
   488→    nheads = config["num_heads"]
   489→
   490→    # Prefill (qseqlen > 1) — fall back to reference
   491→    if qseqlen != 1:
   492→        return ref_kernel(data)
   493→
   494→    total_kv = bs * kvseqlen
   495→
   496→    # Small shapes: Triton flash-decode with bf16 KV
   497→    # Single-pass, no aiter metadata overhead, no reduce kernel
   498→    # Targets: S1 (4k), S3 (32k)
   499→    if total_kv <= TRITON_BF16_THRESHOLD:
   500→        return _triton_bf16_decode(q, kv_data["bf16"], bs, kvseqlen, nheads)
   501→
   502→    # Medium shapes: Triton flash-decode with fp8 KV
   503→    # 2x bandwidth savings, still single-pass
   504→    # Targets: S2 (32k), S5 (64k), S7 (256k)
   505→    if total_kv <= TRITON_FP8_THRESHOLD:
   506→        kv_fp8, kv_scale = kv_data["fp8"]
   507→        return _triton_fp8_decode(q, kv_fp8, kv_scale, bs, kvseqlen, nheads)
   508→
   509→    # Large shapes: aiter ASM with adaptive splits
   510→    # Targets: S4 (256k), S6 (512k), S8 (2M)
   511→    if HAS_AITER:
   512→        return _run_aiter_asm(q, kv_data, qo_indptr, kv_indptr, config)
   513→
   514→    # Ultimate fallback: Triton fp8 (slower but functional)
   515→    kv_fp8, kv_scale = kv_data["fp8"]
   516→    return _triton_fp8_decode(q, kv_fp8, kv_scale, bs, kvseqlen, nheads)
   517→

   114→def _matmul_decode(q, kv_data, bs, kvseqlen, nheads):
   115→    kv = kv_data["bf16"].view(bs, kvseqlen, QK_HEAD_DIM)
   116→    q_3d = q.view(bs, nheads, QK_HEAD_DIM)
   117→    kv_t = kv.transpose(1, 2)
   118→    scores = torch.matmul(q_3d, kv_t).mul_(SM_SCALE)
   119→    weights = torch.softmax(scores, dim=-1)
   120→    v = kv[:, :, :V_HEAD_DIM]
   121→    out = torch.matmul(weights, v)
   122→    return out.unsqueeze(1).reshape(-1, nheads, V_HEAD_DIM)
   123→

   130→

   132→        q_input = q
   133→        q_scale = None
   134→        q_dtype = BF16_DTYPE
   135→    else:

   137→        q_dtype = FP8_DTYPE

### FlashAttention Kernel Implementation in PyTorch

Source: https://github.com/pytorch/helion/blob/main/docs/helion_puzzles.rst

Implements the FlashAttention mechanism using Helion kernels for efficient computation. It processes queries, keys, and values in tiles to optimize cache usage and numerical stability during softmax calculation.

```python
    @helion.kernel()
    def flashatt_kernel(q: torch.Tensor, k: torch.Tensor, v: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
        # Get tensor size
        seq_len = q.size(0)
        # Create output tensor
        out = torch.empty_like(q)

        # Process each query position
        for tile_q in hl.tile(seq_len):
            q_tile = q[tile_q]

            # Initialize tracking variables for stable softmax
            max_val = torch.full_like(q_tile, float('-inf'))
            sum_exp = torch.zeros_like(q_tile)
            weighted_sum = torch.zeros_like(q_tile)

            # Process in tiles for better cache efficiency
            for tile_kv in hl.tile(seq_len):
                k_tile = k[tile_kv]
                v_tile = v[tile_kv]

                # Compute attention scores
                scores = q_tile[:, None] * k_tile[None, :]

                # Find max for numerical stability
                batch_max = torch.amax(scores, dim=1)
                new_max = torch.maximum(max_val, batch_max)

                # Scale old accumulations
                scale_factor = torch.exp(max_val - new_max)
                sum_exp = sum_exp * scale_factor
                weighted_sum = weighted_sum * scale_factor

                # Update with new values
                exp_scores = torch.exp(scores - new_max[:, None])
                sum_exp = sum_exp + torch.sum(exp_scores, dim=1)
                weighted_sum = weighted_sum + torch.sum(exp_scores * v_tile[None, :], dim=1)

                # Update max_val
                max_val = new_max

            # Compute final output
            out[tile_q] = weighted_sum / sum_exp

        return out
```

--------------------------------

### Implement Softmax Kernel with Tensor Creation Functions

Source: https://context7.com/pytorch/helion/llms.txt

Implements a softmax kernel utilizing `hl.zeros()` and `hl.full()` to create accumulator buffers and intermediate tensors within tile loops. These functions are essential for managing temporary storage during complex computations like softmax, which involves finding maximum values and exponentiation.

```python
import torch
import helion
import helion.language as hl

@helion.kernel()
def softmax(x: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
    m, n = x.size()
    out = torch.empty_like(x)

    for tile_m in hl.tile(m):
        # Create accumulator buffers
        max_val = hl.full([tile_m], float("-inf"), dtype=torch.float32)
        sum_exp = hl.zeros([tile_m], dtype=torch.float32)

        # Find max for numerical stability
        for tile_n in hl.tile(n):
            vals = x[tile_m, tile_n].to(torch.float32)
            max_val = torch.maximum(max_val, torch.amax(vals, dim=1))

        # Compute exp and sum
        for tile_n in hl.tile(n):
            vals = x[tile_m, tile_n].to(torch.float32)
            exp_vals = torch.exp(vals - max_val[:, None])
            sum_exp += torch.sum(exp_vals, dim=1)

        # Normalize
        for tile_n in hl.tile(n):
            vals = x[tile_m, tile_n].to(torch.float32)
            exp_vals = torch.exp(vals - max_val[:, None])
            out[tile_m, tile_n] = (exp_vals / sum_exp[:, None]).to(x.dtype)

    return out
```

--------------------------------

### Numerically Optimized Helion Softmax Kernel (Two-Pass)

Source: https://github.com/pytorch/helion/blob/main/notebooks/softmax.ipynb

A Helion kernel implementing a numerically optimized two-pass softmax. This version aims for improved precision by calculating the maximum and sum in separate passes. It utilizes Helion's tiling and register blocking for efficient computation.

```python
from __future__ import annotations
import torch
import helion
from helion._testing import run_example
import helion.language as hl


@helion.kernel(autotune_effort="quick")
def softmax_two_pass(x: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
    """
    Numerically optimized Helion kernel performing softmax in two passes.
    Args:
        x (torch.Tensor): Input tensor of shape [m, n].
    Returns:
        torch.Tensor: Softmax output tensor of the same shape.
    """
    m, n = x.size()
    out = torch.empty_like(x)
    block_size_m = hl.register_block_size(m)
    block_size_n = hl.register_block_size(n)
    for tile_m in hl.tile(m, block_size=block_size_m):
        mi = hl.full([tile_m], float("-inf"), dtype=torch.float32)
        di = hl.zeros([tile_m], dtype=torch.float32)
        for tile_n in hl.tile(n, block_size=block_size_n):
            values = x[tile_m, tile_n]
            local_amax = torch.amax(values, dim=1)
            mi_next = torch.maximum(mi, local_amax)
            di = di * torch.exp(mi - mi_next) + torch.exp(
                values - mi_next[:, None]
            ).sum(dim=1)
            mi = mi_next
        for tile_n in hl.tile(n, block_size=block_size_n):
            values = x[tile_m, tile_n]
            out[tile_m, tile_n] = torch.exp(values - mi[:, None]) / di[:, None]
    return out

def check(m: int, n: int) -> None:
    """
    Runs correctness checks comparing Helion softmax kernels against PyTorch's softmax.
    Args:
        m (int): Number of rows in input tensor.
        n (int): Number of columns in input tensor.
    """
    x = torch.randn([m, n], device="cuda", dtype=torch.float16)
    run_example(softmax_two_pass, lambda x: torch.nn.functional.softmax(x, dim=1), (x,))

def main() -> None:
    """
    Main function to run the softmax kernel correctness check with example input size.
    """
    check(4096, 2560)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

```

### Puzzles > Simple FlashAttention

Source: https://github.com/pytorch/helion/blob/main/docs/helion_puzzles.rst

This puzzle introduces a scalar version of FlashAttention. FlashAttention is an efficient attention mechanism that optimizes the computation of attention scores. This simplified version demonstrates the core idea of calculating attention scores using scaled dot-product, followed by a softmax operation. It involves element-wise multiplication of query and key vectors, finding the maximum value for numerical stability, and then applying the softmax function.

### Puzzles > Long Softmax

Source: https://github.com/pytorch/helion/blob/main/docs/helion_puzzles.rst

This puzzle focuses on implementing the softmax function for a batch of logits using Helion. The `softmax_kernel` computes the softmax of a 2D tensor `x` (batch, sequence length). It performs the computation in three passes: first, it finds the maximum value in each sequence to stabilize the computation; second, it calculates the sum of exponentiated values after subtracting the maximum; and third, it computes the final softmax output by dividing the exponentiated values by the sum. Helion's tiling is used to process the batch dimension efficiently.

     1→"""

     9→
    10→@helion.kernel
    11→def mxfp4_gemm(
    12→    A: torch.Tensor,       # [M, K//2] packed fp4 (uint8)
    13→    A_scale: torch.Tensor, # [M, K//32] e8m0 scales (uint8)
    14→    B: torch.Tensor,       # [N, K//2] packed fp4 (uint8)

    23→            acc = hl.dot_scaled(

    25→                B[tile_n, tile_k], B_scale[tile_n, :], "e2m1",
    26→                acc=acc,
    27→            )
    28→        C[tile_m, tile_n] = acc.to(torch.bfloat16)

     1→"""
     2→Fused MXFP4 Quant + GEMM via tl.dot_scaled on MI355X (gfx950).
     3→
     4→Strategy: Fuse A quantization into the GEMM loop to eliminate the separate
     5→dynamic_mxfp4_quant pass (~10-13µs). Uses the proven _mxfp4_quant_op_precise
     6→JIT function for bit-exact MXFP4 quantization inline with tl.dot_scaled.
     7→
     8→Pre-computes B_t and B_scale once (cached across calls via global dict).
     9→
    10→Target: <15µs geomean across all 5 shapes (down from ~23µs split approach).
    11→"""
    12→
    13→import torch
    14→import triton
    15→import triton.language as tl
    16→from task import input_t, output_t
    17→
    18→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    19→# Inline MXFP4 quantization (bit-exact match to aiter C++ backend)
    20→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    21→
    22→@triton.jit
    23→def _mxfp4_quant_op_precise(x, BLOCK_K, BLOCK_M, QUANT_GROUP: tl.constexpr):
    24→    """Quantize bf16 tile to MXFP4 (fp4x2 packed + e8m0 scales).
    25→
    26→    Args:
    27→        x: [BLOCK_M, BLOCK_K] bf16 tile
    28→        BLOCK_K: K-dimension tile size (constexpr)
    29→        BLOCK_M: M-dimension tile size (constexpr)
    30→        QUANT_GROUP: quantization group size, always 32 (constexpr)
    31→
    32→    Returns:
    33→        x_fp4: [BLOCK_M, BLOCK_K // 2] uint8 packed fp4x2
    34→        scales: [BLOCK_M, BLOCK_K // QUANT_GROUP] uint8 e8m0
    35→    """
    36→    NUM_GROUPS: tl.constexpr = BLOCK_K // QUANT_GROUP
    37→
    38→    # Reshape into quant groups: [M, num_groups, 32]
    39→    x_grouped = tl.reshape(x, [BLOCK_M, NUM_GROUPS, QUANT_GROUP])
    40→
    41→    # Compute per-group amax and derive e8m0 scale
    42→    amax = tl.max(tl.abs(x_grouped), axis=2)
    43→    u32 = amax.to(tl.int32, bitcast=True)
    44→    exponent = ((u32 >> 23) & 0xFF)
    45→    # Round-up logic matching aiter C++ exactly
    46→    round_case = ((u32 & 0x400000) != 0) & (((u32 & 0x3FFFFF) != 0) | (exponent > 0))
    47→    exp_val = tl.where(round_case, exponent + 1, exponent)
    48→    scale_unbiased = exp_val - 129
    49→    bs_e8m0 = (scale_unbiased + 127).to(tl.uint8)
    50→
    51→    # Quantize: scale input, then map to 4-bit codes
    52→    quant_scale_inv = tl.exp2(-scale_unbiased.to(tl.float32))
    53→    qx = x_grouped * quant_scale_inv[:, :, None]
    54→    sign = (qx < 0).to(tl.int32)
    55→    x_abs = tl.abs(qx)
    56→    mag = tl.where(x_abs < 0.25, 0,
    57→          tl.where(x_abs < 0.75, 1,
    58→          tl.where(x_abs < 1.25, 2,
    59→          tl.where(x_abs < 1.75, 3,
    60→          tl.where(x_abs < 2.5, 4,
    61→          tl.where(x_abs < 3.5, 5,
    62→          tl.where(x_abs < 5.0, 6, 7)))))))
    63→    codes = (sign << 3) | mag
    64→
    65→    # Pack adjacent pairs into fp4x2 bytes
    66→    codes_flat = tl.reshape(codes, [BLOCK_M, BLOCK_K])
    67→    idx_k = tl.arange(0, BLOCK_K)
    68→    shift = tl.where(idx_k % 2 == 1, 4, 0)
    69→    shifted = (codes_flat & 0xF) << shift[None, :]
    70→    x_fp4 = tl.sum(
    71→        tl.reshape(shifted, [BLOCK_M, BLOCK_K // 2, 2]), axis=2
    72→    ).to(tl.uint8)
    73→
    74→    return x_fp4, tl.reshape(bs_e8m0, [BLOCK_M, NUM_GROUPS])
    75→
    76→
    77→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    78→# Fused MXFP4 GEMM kernel with autotune
    79→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    80→
    81→def _make_configs():
    82→    """Generate autotune configs covering all M sizes.
    83→
    84→    Small M (4-16): BLOCK_M=16, wider N tiles for bandwidth
    85→    Medium M (64): BLOCK_M=32 or 64
    86→    Large M (256): BLOCK_M=64 or 128 for compute density
    87→    """
    88→    configs = []
    89→    for block_m in [16, 32, 64, 128]:
    90→        for block_n in [64, 128, 256]:
    91→            for block_k in [128, 256]:
    92→                for num_warps in [4, 8]:
    93→                    for num_stages in [1, 2]:
    94→                        # Skip configs that are clearly wasteful
    95→                        # Very large BLOCK_M with small actual M wastes registers
    96→                        if block_m == 128 and block_n == 256:
    97→                            continue  # Too much register pressure
    98→                        if block_k == 256 and block_n == 256:
    99→                            continue  # Exceeds shared memory
   100→                        configs.append(
   101→                            triton.Config(
   102→                                {
   103→                                    "BLOCK_M": block_m,
   104→                                    "BLOCK_N": block_n,
   105→                                    "BLOCK_K": block_k,
   106→                                    "GROUP_SIZE_M": 8,
   107→                                },
   108→                                num_warps=num_warps,
   109→                                num_stages=num_stages,
   110→                            )
   111→                        )
   112→    return configs
   113→
   114→
   115→@triton.autotune(configs=_make_configs(), key=["M", "N", "K"])
   116→@triton.jit
   117→def _fused_mxfp4_gemm_kernel(
   118→    A_ptr, B_ptr, C_ptr, B_scale_ptr,
   119→    M, N, K,
   120→    stride_am, stride_ak,
   121→    stride_bk, stride_bn,
   122→    stride_cm, stride_cn,
   123→    stride_bs_n, stride_bs_k,
   124→    BLOCK_M: tl.constexpr,
   125→    BLOCK_N: tl.constexpr,
   126→    BLOCK_K: tl.constexpr,
   127→    GROUP_SIZE_M: tl.constexpr,
   128→):
   129→    """Fused A-quant + MXFP4 GEMM using tl.dot_scaled.
   130→
   131→    - A is loaded as bf16, quantized to MXFP4 inline per K-tile
   132→    - B is pre-quantized (fp4x2 packed, transposed to [K//2, N])
   133→    - B_scale is pre-unshuffled raw e8m0 [N, K//32]
   134→    - Group-M swizzle for L2 cache locality across CUs
   135→    """
   136→    QUANT_GROUP: tl.constexpr = 32
   137→    SCALES_PER_K_TILE: tl.constexpr = BLOCK_K // QUANT_GROUP
   138→
   139→    # -- Group-M swizzle for L2 locality --
   140→    pid = tl.program_id(0)
   141→    num_blocks_m = tl.cdiv(M, BLOCK_M)
   142→    num_blocks_n = tl.cdiv(N, BLOCK_N)
   143→    num_blocks_in_group = GROUP_SIZE_M * num_blocks_n
   144→    group_id = pid // num_blocks_in_group
   145→    first_pid_m = group_id * GROUP_SIZE_M
   146→    group_size_m = min(num_blocks_m - first_pid_m, GROUP_SIZE_M)
   147→    pid_m = first_pid_m + ((pid % num_blocks_in_group) % group_size_m)
   148→    pid_n = (pid % num_blocks_in_group) // group_size_m
   149→
   150→    # -- Offsets --
   151→    offs_m = pid_m * BLOCK_M + tl.arange(0, BLOCK_M)
   152→    offs_n = pid_n * BLOCK_N + tl.arange(0, BLOCK_N)
   153→    mask_m = offs_m[:, None] < M
   154→    mask_n = offs_n[None, :] < N
   155→
   156→    acc = tl.zeros([BLOCK_M, BLOCK_N], dtype=tl.float32)
   157→
   158→    # -- K-loop: load A bf16, quant inline, dot_scaled with pre-quant B --
   159→    for k_start in range(0, K, BLOCK_K):
   160→        offs_k = k_start + tl.arange(0, BLOCK_K)
   161→
   162→        # Load A tile [BLOCK_M, BLOCK_K] as bf16
   163→        a_bf16 = tl.load(
   164→            A_ptr + offs_m[:, None] * stride_am + offs_k[None, :] * stride_ak,
   165→            mask=mask_m & (offs_k[None, :] < K),
   166→            other=0.0,
   167→        )
   168→
   169→        # Inline MXFP4 quantization of A tile
   170→        a_fp4, a_scale = _mxfp4_quant_op_precise(
   171→            a_bf16, BLOCK_K, BLOCK_M, QUANT_GROUP
   172→        )
   173→
   174→        # Load B tile [BLOCK_K // 2, BLOCK_N] (pre-transposed fp4x2)
   175→        offs_k_packed = (k_start // 2) + tl.arange(0, BLOCK_K // 2)
   176→        b_q = tl.load(
   177→            B_ptr + offs_k_packed[:, None] * stride_bk + offs_n[None, :] * stride_bn,
   178→            mask=(offs_k_packed[:, None] < (K // 2)) & mask_n,
   179→            other=0,
   180→        )
   181→
   182→        # Load B scale tile [BLOCK_N, SCALES_PER_K_TILE] (raw e8m0)
   183→        offs_scale_k = (k_start // QUANT_GROUP) + tl.arange(0, SCALES_PER_K_TILE)
   184→        b_scale = tl.load(
   185→            B_scale_ptr + offs_n[:, None] * stride_bs_n + offs_scale_k[None, :] * stride_bs_k,
   186→            mask=mask_n.T & (offs_scale_k[None, :] < (K // QUANT_GROUP)),
   187→            other=0,
   188→        )
   189→
   190→        # Fused scaled dot product (MXFP4 x MXFP4)
   191→        acc = tl.dot_scaled(
   192→            a_fp4, a_scale, "e2m1",
   193→            b_q, b_scale, "e2m1",
   194→            acc=acc,
   195→        )
   196→
   197→    # -- Store result --
   198→    tl.store(
   199→        C_ptr + offs_m[:, None] * stride_cm + offs_n[None, :] * stride_cn,
   200→        acc.to(tl.bfloat16),
   201→        mask=mask_m & mask_n,
   202→    )
   203→
   204→
   205→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   206→# B preprocessing: unshuffle + transpose (cached across calls)
   207→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   208→
   209→_precomputed_cache: dict[int, tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]] = {}
   210→
   211→
   212→def _unshuffle_e8m0(scale_sh: torch.Tensor, n: int, k: int) -> torch.Tensor:
   213→    """Reverse the aiter e8m0 shuffle to get raw [N, K//32] scales."""
   214→    u8 = scale_sh.view(torch.uint8)
   215→    sm, sn = u8.shape
   216→    s = u8.view(sm // 32, sn // 8, 4, 16, 2, 2)
   217→    s = s.permute(0, 5, 3, 1, 4, 2).contiguous()
   218→    return s.view(sm, sn)[:n, : k // 32]
   219→
   220→
   221→def _get_preprocessed_b(
   222→    B_q: torch.Tensor, B_scale_sh: torch.Tensor, n: int, k: int
   223→) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
   224→    """Get transposed B and unshuffled B_scale, cached by data_ptr."""
   225→    cache_key = B_q.data_ptr()
   226→    if cache_key not in _precomputed_cache:
   227→        B_t = B_q.view(torch.uint8).t().contiguous()
   228→        B_scale = _unshuffle_e8m0(B_scale_sh, n, k)
   229→        _precomputed_cache[cache_key] = (B_t, B_scale)
   230→    return _precomputed_cache[cache_key]
   231→
   232→
   233→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   234→# Entry point
   235→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   236→
   237→def custom_kernel(data: input_t) -> output_t:
   238→    A, B, B_q, B_shuffle, B_scale_sh = data
   239→    m, k = A.shape
   240→    n = B.shape[0]
   241→
   242→    B_t, B_scale = _get_preprocessed_b(B_q, B_scale_sh, n, k)
   243→
   244→    C = torch.empty(m, n, dtype=torch.bfloat16, device=A.device)
   245→
   246→    grid = lambda META: (
   247→        triton.cdiv(m, META["BLOCK_M"]) * triton.cdiv(n, META["BLOCK_N"]),
   248→    )
   249→
   250→    _fused_mxfp4_gemm_kernel[grid](
   251→        A, B_t, C, B_scale,
   252→        m, n, k,
   253→        A.stride(0), A.stride(1),
   254→        B_t.stride(0), B_t.stride(1),
   255→        C.stride(0), C.stride(1),
   256→        B_scale.stride(0), B_scale.stride(1),
   257→    )
   258→
   259→    return C
   260→
```

     1→# Luma AMD Speedrun: Phase 15 — Compound Custom Kernels
     2→
     3→## Context
     4→
     5→We're at the Python dispatch ceiling across all three kernels. 14 phases of optimization have exhausted every Python-level API path. The only way forward is custom kernels compiled on the runner itself.
     6→
     7→**Critical prior learnings (vault + skills):**
     8→- amdclang++ → .so → ctypes pipeline WORKS (confirmed 2026-03-15)
     9→- Naive HIP attention kernel = **19-100x REGRESSION** — kernel quality >> dispatch overhead
    10→- GEMM HIP quant kernel works but has **wrong E8M0 rounding** (±1 scale mismatch in ~30% of groups)
    11→- Bit-accurate E8M0 logic EXISTS in `submission_breakthrough_v9.py` (IEEE 754 bitwise)
    12→- VGPR budget: `acc[32,512]` fp32 = 256 VGPRs = entire budget. Must use split-D or smaller tiles.
    13→- Helion generates correct `tl.dot_scaled` patterns for MXFP4 GEMM
    14→
    15→### Current Standings (Phase 14 best)
    16→
    17→| Kernel | Our Time | Leader | Gap | Status |
    18→|--------|----------|--------|-----|--------|
    19→| MLA | 74.3 µs | 4.3 µs | 17x | Phase 14 OR routing + 3D matmul |
    20→| GEMM | ~23 µs | 9.7 µs | 2.4x | dynamic_mxfp4_quant + gemm_a4w4 |
    21→| MoE | ~155 µs | 145 µs | 1.07x | Adaptive KSPLIT (API ceiling) |
    22→
    23→## Strategy: Local-First Development on Our Silicon
    24→
    25→**Key assets:**
    26→- **Radeon 8060S** (RDNA 4 iGPU) + **128GB unified LPDDR5X** — run Triton/Helion kernels locally for correctness
    27→- **Helion v0.3.3** installed as wheel — generate `tl.dot_scaled` kernels, iterate locally
    28→- **aiter** installed locally — run `dynamic_mxfp4_quant` reference, inspect MLA dispatch source
    29→- **ROCm toolchain** — compile HIP C++ locally for syntax validation
    30→
    31→Triton/Helion generates architecture-portable IR — we validate correctness locally, submit to MI355X only for final timing. This eliminates the slow submit→wait→read-stdout→fix loop.
    32→
    33→Two parallel GEMM tracks + one MLA track, all validated locally before submission:
    34→
    35→### Track A: HIP C++ Quant → ASM GEMM (HIGH confidence, ~17-26% improvement)
    36→
    37→**Problem:** HIP quant kernel has wrong E8M0 rounding (±1 scale byte in ~30% of groups).
    38→
    39→**Solution:** Port IEEE 754 bitwise E8M0 from `submission_breakthrough_v9.py:17-22` to HIP C++.
    40→```c
    41→// IEEE 754 bitwise: extract exponent, round-up-on-tie
    42→unsigned int u32 = __float_as_uint(group_max);
    43→int exponent = (u32 >> 23) & 0xFF;
    44→int round_up = ((u32 & 0x400000) != 0) && (((u32 & 0x3FFFFF) != 0) || (exponent > 0));
    45→int exp_val = round_up ? exponent + 1 : exponent;
    46→scale_byte = (unsigned char)(exp_val - 2);
    47→scale_factor = exp2f((float)(exp_val - 129));
    48→```
    49→
    50→**Local validation:** Pure Python/numpy implementation of the same math, compared bit-for-bit against `dynamic_mxfp4_quant` output. Math bug → math fix → validate locally → port to HIP C++.
    51→
    52→**Pipeline:** HIP quant (~3-5µs) → e8m0_shuffle (~1µs) → gemm_a4w4 ASM (~15µs) = **~19-21µs**
    53→
    54→### Track B: Helion Fused Quant+GEMM (MEDIUM confidence, potentially better than Track A)
    55→
    56→**Problem:** Track A still has a 3-step pipeline. A single fused kernel eliminates intermediate buffers and launch overhead.
    57→
    58→**Solution:** Use Helion to generate a `tl.dot_scaled` Triton kernel with inline MXFP4 quantization — one kernel launch does quant + GEMM.
    59→
    60→**Local validation:** Run the Helion-generated Triton kernel on our Radeon 8060S for correctness. The `tl.dot_scaled` with `'e2m1'` format is portable — same Triton IR, different backend. Verify output matches reference GEMM result on local GPU.
    61→
    62→**Why this could beat Track A:** Phase 6 showed Triton was ~68% slower for raw GEMM — but that was GEMM-only. A fused kernel eliminates: (1) HIP quant launch, (2) e8m0_shuffle, (3) intermediate A_q allocation, (4) gemm_a4w4 launch overhead. If those 4 overheads total >68% of the GEMM time, fusion wins.
    63→
    64→**Compound synergy:** Track A's bit-accurate E8M0 math feeds directly into Track B's inline quant logic. Same math, different packaging.
    65→
    66→### Track C: MLA — C++ Bridge to aiter ASM (MEDIUM confidence, 5-10x improvement)
    67→
    68→**Problem:** 74µs MLA includes ~50µs of Python overhead. ASM kernels run in ~4-10µs.

    70→**Solution:** `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` C++ bridge calling aiter's ASM directly.

    73→
    74→**No fallback.** If the bridge fails, capture error, diagnose, fix.
    75→

I now have a thorough understanding of the codebase. Let me compile the comprehensive plan.

Here is the comprehensive Phase 16 plan for the Luma AMD Speedrun competition, based on my thorough exploration of the codebase:

---

## Phase 16 Plan: Helion-Generated Triton Kernels

### Context: Why Helion Is the Breakthrough

Every approach tried in Phases 1-15 was a Python-level dispatch optimization on top of existing library kernels. The current submissions call into aiter's pre-compiled ASM/CK kernels (`gemm_a4w4`, `mla_decode_stage1_asm_fwd`, `fused_moe`), adding layers of Python orchestration, quantization passes, and routing logic. This hit a performance wall:

- **GEMM at 20.8us**: The 3-step pipeline (Triton quant + e8m0_shuffle + ASM gemm_a4w4) has irreducible launch overhead. The leader at 9.7us almost certainly uses a single fused kernel.
- **MLA at 72us**: The multi-regime routing (matmul / aiter a16w8 / aiter a8w8) with metadata computation, split-K reduce, and buffer pre-allocation can never approach 4.3us. The leader uses a single fused flash-decode kernel.
- **MoE at 155us**: Closest to the leader (145us, 1.07x gap), but still limited by aiter's tuning choices.

The hand-written fused Triton kernels (`submission_fused_triton.py` at 68% slower for GEMM, `submission_triton_flash.py` at ~130us for MLA) proved the architecture is right but the tile sizes and scheduling are wrong. Helion's evolutionary autotuner can search the space that manual config selection cannot.

**Critical workflow constraint**: Helion (v0.3.2) is installed locally at `.venv-helion/` but is NOT on the competition runner. We generate Triton code locally via `bound.to_triton_code()`, extract the `@triton.jit` kernel, and submit it as standalone Triton.

---

### Track 1: GEMM -- Helion Fused Quant+GEMM (20.8us to ~10us target)

**Current state**: `kernels/mxfp4-mm/submission.py` uses HIP C++ quantization + `gemm_a4w4` ASM. Already have `helion_gemm_gen.py` that generates Triton code for fp4xfp4 GEMM using `hl.dot_scaled`.

**Why current approach is slow**: Three kernel launches (quant ~5-8us, e8m0_shuffle ~1us, gemm_a4w4 ~12us) plus intermediate memory allocations. The leader fuses everything into one kernel launch.

#### Task 1.1: Enhance Helion GEMM codegen script

The existing `helion_gemm_gen.py` assumes both A and B are pre-quantized fp4. For the competition, A arrives as bf16 and needs inline quantization. The existing `submission_fused_triton.py` already has the inline quant logic (`_mxfp4_quant_op_precise`) but uses hand-tuned configs.

**Subtasks**:
1. Create `helion_fused_gemm_gen.py` that takes bf16 A input and uses `hl.dot_scaled` for the GEMM portion
2. The A quantization must happen per-K-tile inside the K-loop. Helion cannot express inline quantization natively, so use `hl.inline_triton()` to embed the quantization logic as raw Triton within the Helion kernel
3. Alternative approach: Generate the GEMM-only kernel via Helion (with pre-quantized A), then manually prepend the quantization logic to the generated Triton code. This avoids fighting Helion's DSL limitations

**Design decision**: Approach 3 (generate GEMM via Helion, manually fuse quant) is more reliable. The `_mxfp4_quant_op_precise` JIT function from `submission_fused_triton.py` is already proven correct. Helion's value is in finding optimal tile sizes for the `tl.dot_scaled` call, not in expressing the quant logic.

#### Task 1.2: Generate Triton code for all 5 competition shapes

The competition has 5 shapes with M variable, K=7168, N=1024 (or K=1024, N=7168). Run Helion codegen for each shape configuration:

**Subtasks**:
1. Enumerate competition shapes from the test spec (M values: small like 4-16, medium like 64, large like 256)
2. For each shape, call `mxfp4_gemm.bind(args)` with appropriate `helion.Config` settings
3. Use Helion's autotuner (no config = evolutionary search) OR test multiple explicit configs
4. Extract generated Triton code via `bound.to_triton_code()`
5. Since Helion can run on CPU for codegen, this step works locally without GPU

#### Task 1.3: Assemble submission file

**Subtasks**:
1. Take the Helion-generated `@triton.jit` kernel
2. Wrap it with `@triton.autotune` using the configs that Helion identified as optimal
3. Add the `_mxfp4_quant_op_precise` JIT function as a called helper
4. Add the B preprocessing (unshuffle + transpose from `submission_fused_triton.py` lines 209-230)
5. Add `custom_kernel` entry point matching competition interface
6. Validate correctness against reference kernel before benchmarking

#### Task 1.4: Iterative tuning on runner

**Subtasks**:
1. Submit for correctness test first (`--mode test`)
2. If correct, submit for benchmark (`--mode benchmark`)
3. Analyze per-shape timing breakdowns
4. If specific shapes are slow, generate shape-specific Helion configs
5. Consider generating multiple Triton kernels (one per M-size regime) with Python routing in `custom_kernel`

---

### Track 2: MLA -- Helion Flash-Decode (72us to ~4-10us target)

**Current state**: `kernels/mixed-mla/submission.py` uses aiter's ASM MLA decode with three-regime routing. The hand-written Triton flash-decode (`submission_triton_flash.py`) ran at ~130us.

**Why current approach is slow**: aiter's MLA path requires metadata computation (`get_mla_metadata_v1`), two kernel launches (stage1 ASM + reduce), buffer allocations, and FP8 quantization of Q. The leader at 4.3us skips all of this with a single flash-decode kernel.

**Why the hand-written Triton was slow**: Head dim 576 (non-power-of-2) causes VGPR pressure. The kernel pads to 640, wasting 64 elements per head. The online softmax loop uses element-wise operations (`tl.sum(q[None, :] * k, axis=1)`) instead of `tl.dot`, which means no matrix engine utilization.

#### Task 2.1: Design Helion flash-decode kernel

**Key insight**: MLA uses head_dim=576 for QK and head_dim=512 for V. The QK dot product is Q[1, 576] @ K[BLOCK_N, 576]^T = scores[1, BLOCK_N]. This is NOT a matrix multiply in the traditional sense (M=1). It's a vector-matrix product (decode, qseqlen=1).

**Design approach**:
1. Grid: (batch_size, num_heads) -- one program per (batch, head)
2. Outer loop: tile over KV sequence length with `hl.tile(kvseqlen)`
3. Per tile: compute QK scores, online softmax, accumulate weighted V
4. Helion CAN express this using `hl.dot()` for the QK scores if we reshape appropriately
5. For V accumulation: `p[BLOCK_N, 1] * V[BLOCK_N, 512]` can use `hl.dot()` with fp32 accumulator

**Subtasks**:
1. Write Helion kernel that expresses flash-decode with `hl.tile()` for KV sequence
2. Handle the 576-dim issue: let Helion decide padding strategy (it may pad to 640 or 1024)
3. For the softmax, use standard PyTorch ops within Helion (`torch.max`, `torch.exp`, `torch.sum`) which Helion lowers to Triton reductions
4. For the QK dot product, reshape Q from [576] to [1, 576] and use `hl.dot(Q_tile, K_tile.T)`

**Critical challenge**: Helion's `hl.dot()` requires both operands to be 2D with compatible dimensions. For decode (qseqlen=1), the M dimension of the dot is 1, which may be below Helion's minimum block size. We may need to use `hl.inline_triton()` for the QK computation, or use element-wise multiply + reduce instead of dot.

#### Task 2.2: Handle the two KV cache formats

The competition provides three KV formats: bf16, fp8 (with scale), and mxfp4. The leader likely uses fp8 for bandwidth savings.

**Subtasks**:
1. Generate two kernel variants: one for bf16 KV, one for fp8 KV
2. For fp8: fold `kv_scale` into `sm_scale` for QK computation, apply to V after normalization (same as `submission_triton_flash.py` approach)
3. Route based on `total_kv`: bf16 for small (better precision), fp8 for large (better bandwidth)
4. Consider mxfp4 path: use `hl.dot_scaled()` with KV cache in fp4 format. This would be a breakthrough if it works (4x bandwidth savings vs bf16)

#### Task 2.3: Generate and extract Triton code

**Subtasks**:
1. Run Helion codegen for each of the 8 competition shapes
2. Extract generated Triton kernels
3. Add `@triton.autotune` wrappers with Helion-discovered configs
4. Test correctness against reference kernel

#### Task 2.4: Shape-specific optimization

The 8 MLA shapes span a huge range (bs=1 kvseqlen=4096 to bs=32 kvseqlen=65536). Different shapes need different strategies:

**Subtasks**:
1. Small total_kv (4k-32k): Single program per (batch, head), no split-K needed. Triton kernel should dominate
2. Medium total_kv (32k-256k): May need split-K parallelism. Helion can express this with multiple tile levels
3. Large total_kv (256k-2M): MUST beat aiter ASM. If Triton can't, we keep aiter for this regime (but the user said no fallbacks, so this is a risk)

---

### Track 3: MoE -- Conditional on Tracks 1 and 2 (155us to ~145us target)

**Current state**: `kernels/moe-mxfp4/submission.py` uses aiter's `fused_moe` with adaptive KSPLIT. Gap is only 1.07x.

**Decision**: Only pursue if Tracks 1 and 2 succeed. The 10us savings here has far less ROI than the GEMM (11us gap) and MLA (68us gap).

If pursued:
1. Write Helion kernel for expert GEMM with mxfp4 weights via `hl.dot_scaled`
2. Fuse expert routing + GEMM to avoid per-expert dispatch
3. Generate Triton code, extract, submit

---

### Development Workflow (applies to all tracks)

1. **Write Helion kernel** in a new `.py` file under `research/challenges/luma_amd_speedrun/`
2. **Activate Helion venv**: `source .venv-helion/bin/activate`
3. **Run codegen**: `python helion_<kernel>_gen.py` -- this works on CPU, no GPU needed
4. **Extract Triton code** from stdout/file output
5. **Create submission file**: Add competition boilerplate, B preprocessing, `custom_kernel` entry
6. **Submit for test**: `popcorn-cli submit --mode test` to verify correctness
7. **Submit for benchmark**: `popcorn-cli submit --mode benchmark` for timing
8. **Iterate**: If slow, adjust Helion configs or manually tune the generated Triton

---

### Verification

#### Correctness verification
- Each generated kernel MUST pass the competition's correctness check (`--mode test`)
- Cross-validate against the reference kernel for all 5 GEMM shapes and 8 MLA shapes
- Compare outputs against current submissions (should be numerically identical or better)

#### Performance verification
- Target geomean improvements: GEMM 20.8us to ~10us (2x), MLA 72us to <20us (3.6x+)
- Submit via `--mode benchmark` after correctness passes
- Compare individual shape timings against current best

#### Risk checkpoints
- After Task 1.2: If Helion cannot generate valid `tl.dot_scaled` code, fall back to using Helion for tile size search only, keeping the hand-written kernel structure
- After Task 2.1: If Helion's minimum block sizes prevent M=1 decode, use `hl.inline_triton()` to embed the QK computation
- After Task 2.4: If large shapes (>256k total_kv) are still slower than aiter ASM, this is the one place where we may need to accept aiter for that single regime

---

### Risks (honest assessment)

1. **Helion's `dot_scaled` may not generate optimal tl.dot_scaled for gfx950**: Helion was built for NVIDIA GPUs. The `tl.dot_scaled` on AMD/gfx950 has specific requirements (scale layout, packed fp4 format) that Helion may not handle correctly. Mitigation: Use Helion for tile size discovery only, keep hand-written kernel structure.

2. **Helion autotuner requires GPU for tuning**: While codegen works on CPU, the evolutionary search (`DifferentialEvolution`) needs to actually run the kernel. We need GPU access for autotuning. Mitigation: Use manual configs informed by the hand-tuned `submission_fused_triton.py` configs, or run autotuning on a remote GPU.

3. **MLA head_dim=576 may defeat Helion's tile strategy**: 576 = 64 * 9, which is not a power of 2. Helion may pad to 1024 (wasting 44% of register space) or refuse to compile. Mitigation: Use `hl.inline_triton()` for the head-dim operations.

4. **Generated Triton code may need manual editing**: Helion's output may include Helion-specific imports or runtime calls that aren't available on the runner. Mitigation: Carefully extract only the `@triton.jit` function and its dependencies.

5. **Scale tensor layout mismatch**: aiter's e8m0 scales use a shuffled layout. Helion's `dot_scaled` expects raw `[M, K//32]` layout. The B preprocessing (unshuffle) from `submission_fused_triton.py` handles this, but the A quantization must produce the right layout inline. This was already solved in the existing fused kernel.

---

> AGENT

Excellent research from all agents. Now let me synthesize this into the final plan. The key insight that emerged: Helion's value isn't just code generation — it's the **autotuner**. The hand-written fused Triton kernels had the right architecture but wrong tile sizes. Helion's DifferentialEvolution search across 1500+ configs is what the winners used (via manual Triton autotune or similar).

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**Why the hand-written Triton flash-decode was 130µs while the leader is 4.3µs:**
1. The kernel used element-wise `tl.sum(q * k, axis=1)` instead of `tl.dot()` — no matrix engine utilization
2. Head dim 576 padded to 640 wastes VGPR budget on zero elements
3. `BLOCK_N=64/128` was hand-picked, not autotuned for MI355X's 256 CUs
4. No `num_stages` pipelining — memory latency not hidden

Helion solves all four: it uses `torch.matmul` → `tl.dot`, handles padding internally, and autotunes `block_sizes`, `num_warps`, `num_stages`, and `loop_orders` via evolutionary search.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**The plan's core bet**: Helion's evolutionary autotuner searching 1500+ configs can find tile sizes that hand-tuning missed. The existing `submission_fused_triton.py` has the correct kernel architecture (fused quant + `tl.dot_scaled`) but was 68% slower than ASM with hand-picked configs. If Helion finds configs that close even half that gap, the fusion savings (eliminating 3 launches + intermediate allocations) push us past the ASM pipeline.

For MLA, the bet is bigger: replacing aiter's entire 2-stage pipeline (metadata + ASM + reduce) with a single flash-decode Triton kernel. The key architectural change is using `tl.dot` instead of element-wise `tl.sum(q * k)` — this engages MI355X's MFMA matrix engine instead of scalar ALUs.

Scalar online softmax accumulators — not vectorized\n\n**Helion fixes all of these**: It lowers `torch.matmul` → `tl.dot` (MFMA), handles padding, autotunes everything.\n\n### Task 2.1: Write Helion flash-decode kernel\n- [ ] Create `helion_mla_flash_gen.py` with `@helion.kernel()` flash-decode\n- [ ] Structure: `hl.tile(seq_len)` for KV sequence, online softmax per tile\n- [ ] Use `torch.matmul(q_tile, k_tile.T)` for QK scores — Helion lowers to `tl.dot`\n- [ ] Use `torch.matmul(p_tile.unsqueeze(0), v_tile)` for weighted V — Helion lowers to `tl.dot`\n- [ ] Online softmax: `torch.amax`, `torch.exp`, running max/sum as in Helion's FlashAttention puzzle\n- [ ] Handle QK_HEAD_DIM=576, V_HEAD_DIM=512: use `hl.register_block_size()` to control padding\n\n### Task 2.2: Generate kernels for both KV formats\n- [ ] **bf16 KV variant**: Direct flash-decode, best for small shapes\n- [ ] **fp8 KV variant**: Fold `kv_scale` into `sm_scale` for QK, apply to V after normalization\n- [ ] Route by `total_kv`: bf16 for small (≤32k), fp8 for large (>32k)\n- [ ] Consider: single kernel handling both via dtype dispatch (fewer Triton compilations on runner)\n\n### Task 2.3: Extract and assemble submission\n- [ ] Run Helion autotuner for each of the 8 competition shapes\n- [ ] Extract generated `@triton.jit` kernels\n- [ ] Add `@triton.autotune` with Helion-discovered configs\n- [ ] Wire into `custom_kernel(data)` with competition interface unpacking\n- [ ] No aiter imports. No metadata. No split-K. `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**AMD Ryzen AI MAX+ 395 with Radeon 8060S (RDNA 4 iGPU) + 128GB unified LPDDR5X** is a unique asset:

1. **Unified memory architecture**: The iGPU shares 128GB with the CPU. No PCIe transfer bottleneck. We can run massive autotuning sweeps without running out of VRAM — Helion's DifferentialEvolution search across 1500+ configs needs to allocate/deallocate kernel buffers rapidly.

2. **RDNA 4 has Triton support**: ROCm + Triton works on RDNA 4 (gfx1200). We can actually **run** Helion kernels locally for correctness verification, not just codegen. The Triton IR is portable — same kernel compiles for both RDNA 4 (local) and CDNA 4/gfx950 (runner).

3. **AVX-512 + AMX on the CPU**: The 16C/32T CPU can run Helion's autotuner coordination (which is CPU-bound Python) while the iGPU runs kernel trials in parallel. Autotuning is embarrassingly parallel within the evolutionary search.

This is very revealing. Let me update the plan with what the local silicon enables.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**What we found — local silicon capabilities:**

1. **ROCm 7.2.0 installed** with **gfx1151 (RDNA 4) rocblas kernels** — our Radeon 8060S iGPU is fully supported by ROCm
2. **Triton 3.6.0** available in the Helion venv — but torch is CPU-only (`2.10.0+cpu`)
3. **ROCm libs exist** at `/opt/rocm/lib/` with `hipcc` available
4. **The project venv has CUDA torch** (`2.9.1+cu128`) which won't see our AMD GPU

**The unique advantage**: If we install ROCm-enabled PyTorch in the Helion venv, we can **actually run and autotune Helion kernels on gfx1151**. Helion's autotuner (DifferentialEvolution, 1500+ configs, ~10 minutes) needs a real GPU to benchmark candidates. With a local GPU, we:
- Run the full autotuner locally (vs guessing configs on CPU)
- Verify correctness locally before submitting to MI355X
- Iterate 100x faster than the submit→wait→parse loop

**The catch**: gfx1151 (RDNA 4) has a different ISA than gfx950 (CDNA 4). Tile sizes that are optimal on RDNA 4 may not be optimal on CDNA 4. But Helion's output is Triton IR, which recompiles per-arch. The **optimal configs may transfer** (same memory hierarchy principles), or we use local tuning as a starting point and let `@triton.autotune` on the runner do the final selection.

**128GB unified memory** means we can run autotuning on large shapes without VRAM limits — something impossible on typical 8-16GB discrete GPUs.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`
